Judge — “AgentCore runtime instances are now generally available”13 locations · 3 conclusions flip

verdict
shape changed, not value  on agentcore.session_cap
was AgentCore Runtime session cap: 8h (single value, applies to all AgentCore runtime)
now AgentCore Runtime session cap now dimensioned by compute type: microVM (default, serverless) = 8h (unchanged); EC2-backed runtime instances (new, GA) = up to 14 days, using capacity providers with GPU/memory/compute-optimized EC2 instance families
still true
The 8h session cap remains correct for the default serverless microVM-based AgentCore runtime path.
false positive from the filter
agent-advisor/references/decision-refs/poc-shapes.md
caps applied
[b1] Only hits directly discussing AgentCore's session cap value or judgments/comparisons derived from that cap were treated as affected. Generic mentions of 'AgentCore runtime', 'microVM', or 'Lambda MicroVMs' unrelated to session duration (e.g., SKILL.md triggers, poc-shapes.md artifacts, confirm.md/design.md routing logic, billing notes) are unaffected because they don't state or depend on the specific 8h/session-cap value. The lambda-microvms.md line 17 hit is borderline between value and derived since it repeats the old cap as a comparison basis but its real content is a comparative judgment ('same as...NOT longer') that no longer holds fully true. [b2] All 30 grep hits in this batch are unaffected by the session-cap change. The "[microVM]" hits refer to "Lambda MicroVMs" — a distinct Lambda-based deployment/runtime target used in design/estimate/generate/migration-plan/poc docs for enumerating runtime options (agentcore | lambda_microvms | ecs | eks | lambda | batch | fargate) and cost anchors — this is an unrelated concept from AWS Lambda, not the AgentCore Runtime's microVM-based default execution environment discussed in the announcement. None of these lines state or depend on any session duration/cap value. The "[AgentCore runtime]" hits describe architectural/deployment facts (entrypoint server, container image, POC staging, retarget logic, harness injection) that do not reference or depend on session length/duration at all — they are about routing/POC/deployment mechanics unrelated to the 8h/14-day session cap distinction. Since the change is scoped specifically to session duration limits (agentcore.session_cap), and none of these lines mention session duration, timeouts, or make a duration-dependent recommendation, all are classified unaffected. [b3] Key affected hits: agentcore.json line 8 is a scoring rule that hard-codes the old blanket 8hr cap as disqualifying for AgentCore — this needs to be updated since EC2-backed runtime instances now support up to 14 days, otherwise agents needing long sessions could be wrongly scored against AgentCore. design-ref-agentic-to-agentcore.md line 141 is the clearest 'flipped' case: the recommendation to break tasks into sub-tasks for >8h sessions is a workaround for a limitation that the new EC2-backed runtime instances tier removes. Lines 17 and 130 in the same file state the 8-hour figure as an unqualified fact about AgentCore Runtime generally, which is now incomplete since it's specific to the default microVM path. All lambda_microvms.json and scoring-result.json hits are about a different, unrelated product (Lambda MicroVMs) despite the similar naming, and are unaffected. The poc.md hits reference lambda_microvms infra/deployment mechanics unrelated to the AgentCore session-duration fact. [b4] Batch 4 focuses mainly on clarify-ai.md's task_duration decision table, which is the most directly affected content since it encodes judgments/recommendations keyed to the old flat 8h session cap. Lines 488, 489, and 518 required rewrites: line 489 is a 'flipped' classification because the old recommendation (decompose tasks / use ECS/EKS for >8h tasks) is now reversed — AgentCore Runtime with EC2-backed instances can now handle up to 14-day sessions directly. Lines 488 and 518 are 'derived' since the underlying judgment (recommend AgentCore, or flag session limit issues) needs updated nuance about compute type, but the core recommendation direction doesn't fully reverse. Line 581 is 'derived' since task_duration's role expands to also determine compute type. All other hits (Harness-specific session limits, monitoring/rollback windows, region tables, pricing, deployment script text, and an unrelated Heroku '14 days' replay window) are unaffected because they either reference a different system (Harness, not AgentCore Runtime), a different kind of duration (monitoring/rollback windows), or no session-cap-specific claim at all. Note that Harness's 8-hour limit (line 17 in design-ref-harness.md) is explicitly a Harness session limit, distinct from AgentCore Runtime's session cap discussed in the announcement, so left unaffected despite the surface-level 8-hour match. [b5] The single grep hit in this batch does not relate to AgentCore Runtime session caps — it appears to be a generic interview question option about a '14 days' timeframe unconnected to compute type or session duration limits. No rewrite needed.

 conclusion flips
before
- **AgentCore Runtime**: 8-hour max execution → OUT as Worker host. (Its correct
after

 conclusion flips
before
| `very_long` (1hr+) | AgentCore Runtime with session chaining. Break into sub-tasks if > 8 hours. |

why

clarify-ai.md:489
gcp-to-aws/references/phases/clarify
 conclusion flips
before
| Very long | AgentCore Runtime required (8-hour max session). If tasks exceed 8 hours: recommend breaking into sub-tasks with session chaining, or evaluate custom compute (ECS/EKS). |
after
| Very long | AgentCore Runtime required. Default microVM path is capped at 8 hours; for tasks exceeding 8 hours, recommend EC2-backed runtime instances (GA), which support sessions up to 14 days via capacity providers, instead of defaulting to sub-task decomposition or custom compute (ECS/EKS). |
why
The announcement states EC2-backed runtime instances now support sessions up to 14 days, directly resolving the 'very long' task case that previously required workarounds (sub-tasking or ECS/EKS) due to the old single 8h cap. The recommendation conclusion flips from 'always decompose/migrate off AgentCore' to 'use runtime instances within AgentCore.'

No CI check can catch a badly reworded judgment — the “why” column is the reviewer’s only protection, so it is mandatory.

Judge — “Announcing temporal policies and rate limiting in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore”3 locations · 0 conclusions flip

verdict
 on agentcore.governance_controls
was agentcore.md lists Guardrails: Bedrock Guardrails + Policy (Cedar) for high-risk actions; no rate limiting or temporal (stateful) authorization policy mentioned
now AgentCore Gateway now adds: temporal policies (stateful authorization evaluating requests against prior session actions - workflow sequencing, argument-matches-prior-output, human approval, data freshness) and rate limiting (per-user/per-group, OAuth/IAM-scoped, on requests/tokens/concurrent connections) as new controls alongside existing Guardrails/Policy(Cedar)
still true
Guardrails + Policy (Cedar) for high-risk actions remains valid; this adds new orthogonal controls, not a replacement
false positive from the filter

locationkindproposed change & justification
ai-migration-guardrails.md:95
gcp-to-aws/references/shared
 derived judgment
before
- Recommend Policy when `agentic_profile.tools` contains write-capable tools (database writes, API mutations, file operations) or when the startup has compliance requirements.
after
- Recommend Policy when `agentic_profile.tools` contains write-capable tools (database writes, API mutations, file operations) or when the startup has compliance requirements. For stateful, session-aware controls (workflow sequencing, argument-matches-prior-output, human approval, data freshness) or for per-user/per-group rate limiting on requests, tokens, or concurrent connections, also recommend the new AgentCore Gateway temporal policies and rate limiting controls.
why
Announcement adds temporal policies and rate limiting as new AgentCore Gateway controls alongside existing Policy(Cedar); the recommendation guidance for when to use Policy should now note these additional orthogonal controls for stateful/session-aware and traffic-rate scenarios.

before

after
- Policies are stored in a policy engine attached to AgentCore Gateway. The gateway intercepts agent-tool traffic and evaluates each request before granting or denying access. The gateway now also supports temporal (stateful) authorization policies that evaluate requests against prior session actions, and rate limiting on requests, tokens, and concurrent connections.
why
Announcement states AgentCore Gateway now adds temporal policies and rate limiting as controls; since this line describes what the gateway evaluates/enforces, it is incomplete without mentioning these new capabilities.

No CI check can catch a badly reworded judgment — the “why” column is the reviewer’s only protection, so it is mandatory.

The docs page showed 'Available' while the feature was pre-release. A docs label alone is not GA evidence.

Every number here came from a real run: facts re-fetched from their own source pages, announcements pulled from the live AWS What’s New feed, and all judgments produced by models on Amazon Bedrock. Review itself stays in the pull request — this page exists for what a PR list cannot show.
